\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}-\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(j)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(n)}-\alpha(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(n)})\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(n-1)}-\alpha(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(n)})(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(n-1)})\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(n-2)}

\displaystyle-\ldots-\alpha\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(1)}.

Expanding each product and grouping by powers of
\alpha
, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}-\alpha\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n}\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}+\alpha^{2}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq n}\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{2})}\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}-\alpha^{3}\!\!\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}\leq n}\!\!\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{3})}\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{2})}\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}

\displaystyle+\ldots+(-1)^{n}\alpha^{n}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}\leq n}\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{n})}\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{n-1})}\ldots\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{2})}\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}.

We note that by definition of
\mathbf{M}
,
\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n}\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}=n\mathbf{M}
. Regrouping the remaining terms, we obtain:

\mathbf{H}_{k}=\mathbf{I}-n\alpha\mathbf{M}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}(-1)^{j}\alpha^{j}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n}\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{j})}\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{j-1})}\ldots\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{2})}\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}.

Hence, by the triangle inequality, it can be inferred that

\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{I}-n\alpha\mathbf{M}\right\rvert+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n}\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{j})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{j-1})}\right\rvert\ldots\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{2})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}\right\rvert.

(C.7)

We begin by bounding
\left\lvert\mathbf{I}-n\alpha\mathbf{M}\right\rvert
in a procedure similar to that of Theorem
C.1
. Observe that for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\mathbf{I}-n\alpha\mathbf{M})\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}-n\alpha\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}-n\alpha\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}+n^{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-2n\alpha\mu+n^{2}\alpha^{2}l^{2})\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2},

where the last inequality follows from
\left\lvert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{M}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert\leq l\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert
and
\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}^{T}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}\geq\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}
. As the above holds true for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{I}-n\alpha\mathbf{M}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{1-2n\alpha\mu+n^{2}\alpha^{2}l^{2}}\leq\sqrt{1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{5}}\leq 1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{10},

where we use
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
in the second and last inequalities.

It now remains to bound the right hand side of (
C.7
). Note that the constraint
1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n
implies that there exist only
\binom{n}{j}
possible choices for the
t_{i}
’s. Furthermore, as stated in Step 1, the
l
-smoothness of the components
\mathbf{\omega}_{i}(\mathbf{z})
implies that
\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}\right\rvert,\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l,\forall i\in[n]
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n}\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{j})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{j-1})}\right\rvert\ldots\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t_{2})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(t_{1})}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\binom{n}{j}l^{j}.

Combining the previous inequalities allows us to bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert
as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{10}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}(\alpha l)^{j}\binom{n}{j}\leq 1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{10}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}(\alpha nl)^{j}\leq 1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{10}+\frac{\alpha^{2}n^{2}l^{2}}{1-\alpha nl}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{10}+\frac{5\alpha^{2}n^{2}l^{2}}{4}\leq 1-\frac{9n\alpha\mu}{10}+\frac{n\alpha\mu}{4}\leq 1-n\alpha\mu/2,

where we substitute
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}\leq 1/5
wherever required.

We highlight that our analysis so far holds for any permutation
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Consequently, Steps 1 and 2 directly generalize to the adversarial shuffling regime without any modifications. We use this as a starting point for our analysis of GDA-AS in Theorem
C.3
.

We now proceed to control the magnitude of the noise term
\mathbf{r}_{k}
by bounding its expected squared norm
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
, where the expectation is taken over the single uniform permutation
\tau
for GDA-SO and over independent uniform permutations
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{K}
for GDA-RR.

Step 3: Upper bounding
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

Recall that, as per Step 1,
\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)}\right\rvert\leq l
. Hence, applying the triangle inequality yields,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\right\rvert|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|

\displaystyle\leq l(1+\alpha l)^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|

\displaystyle\leq l(1+\frac{\mu}{5nl})^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\leq le^{1/5}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|.

To bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}
, we use the inequality
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})^{2}\leq n\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}
which follows from Young’s inequality. This yields:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\right)^{2}\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}.

We now convert the above inequality into an upper bound for
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
. For the sake of clarity, we illustrate the case of GDA-RR and GDA-SO separately. However, we remark that the proof for both cases is practically identical and relies on an application of Lemma
B.3
to the term
|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}
.

Step 3A:
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
for GDA-RR

We write
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
to explicitly denote the dependence of the expectation on the uniform random permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
. We then use Lemma
B.3
to bound
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{k}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\sigma_{*}^{2}=l^{2}e^{2/5}\sigma_{*}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i(n-i)

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4,

where
\sigma_{*}^{2}
denotes the gradient variance at the minimax point
\mathbf{z}^{*}
, defined as
\sigma_{*}^{2}=1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{\omega}_{i}(\mathbf{z}^{*})\right\rvert^{2}
. We highlight that the second inequality follows from the independence of the uniformly sampled permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
and the third inequality follows from Lemma
B.3
(recall that
1/n\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{\omega}(\mathbf{z}^{*})=\nu(\mathbf{z}^{*})=0
).

Step 3B:
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
for GDA-SO

For GDA-SO,
\tau_{1}=\ldots=\tau_{K}=\tau
where
\tau
is a uniformly sampled random permutation. Hence, this yields the following:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}.

Proceeding along the same lines as Step 3A, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\sigma_{*}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4.

Hence, for both GDA-RR and GDA-SO,
\mathbf{r}_{k}
is bounded in expectation as
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4
.

Having bounded both
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert
and
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
, we now proceed to present a complete unified proof of convergence for GDA-RR and GDA-SO.

Step 4: Convergence analysis

Unrolling (
C.6
) for
K
epochs and setting
\mathbf{z}^{1}_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{0}
gives us the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{H}_{k}\right](\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k+1}^{K}\mathbf{H}_{j}\right]\mathbf{r}_{k}.

Using the triangle inequality and the bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\leq 1-n\alpha\mu/2
results in:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k+1}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{j}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K-k}\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert.

By applying Young’s inequality, we conclude the following:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}.

Taking expectations (with respect to the uniform random permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
for RR and
\tau
for SO respectively) on both sides of the above inequality and substituting the upper bound on
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
as derived in Step 3 gives us the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\left(l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/2\right)\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}.

By simplifying the last term, we obtain the following guarantee that holds for GDA-RR and GDA-SO whenever
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
,

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{2}K}{\mu}.

(C.8)

To complete the proof, we substitute
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
in (
C.8
).

Under this choice of step-size, using
\alpha\leq\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}
, we bound the second term in the right hand side of (
C.8
) as

\displaystyle\frac{l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{2}K}{\mu}\leq\frac{8l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\mu^{4}}\frac{\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{nK^{2}}.

By substituting the above expression into (
C.8
), we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{8\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{nK^{2}}.

(C.9)

We now consider the following cases:

Case 1:
\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}\leq\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}

By our choice of the step-size,
\alpha=\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
, which implies that

\displaystyle 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}=2e^{-\frac{K}{5\kappa^{2}}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

By substituting into (
C.9
), we obtain the following rate for Case 1,

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-\frac{K}{5\kappa^{2}}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{8\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{nK^{2}}.

(C.10)

Case 2:
\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}

By our choice of the step-size,
\alpha=\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}
. It follows that:

\displaystyle 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}\leq\frac{\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}}\frac{2}{nK^{2}}\leq\frac{2}{nK^{2}},

where the last inequality follows from the
\mu
-strong monotonicity of
\nu
.

Substituting the above inequality into (
C.9
), we obtain the following rate for Case 2,

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq\frac{2\mu^{2}+8\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu^{2}nK^{2}}.

(C.11)

Taking the maximum of the right hand side of (
C.10
) and (
C.11
), we finally obtain the desired last-iterate convergence rate which holds for both cases:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-\frac{K}{5\kappa^{2}}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+8\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu^{2}nK^{2}}.

(C.12)

Suppressing constant terms and logarithmic factors, we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}}).

(C.13)

We highlight that the obtained last-iterate convergence guarantee holds for both GDA-RR and GDA-SO, and is applicable for any number of epochs
K\geq 1
.
∎

Recovering the rates of full-batch GDA

We note that when
n=1
, i.e., in the non-stochastic regime, GDA-RR/SO reduces to full-batch GDA and
\sigma^{2}_{*}=0
. Substituting this into (
C.12
), we note that Theorem
C.2
implies a last-iterate convergence guarantee of
O(\exp(-K/5\kappa^{2}))
, which matches the tight convergence rate of full-batch GDA as established in Theorem
C.1
. Thus, our analysis of GDA-RR/SO automatically recovers the rates of full-batch GDA and the exponential dependence of
-K/5\kappa^{2}
is optimal up to constant factors. We highlight that the above argument holds even when
n>1
as long as
\sigma^{2}_{*}=0
(which occurs whenever
\mathbf{z}^{*}
is the common root of all the operators
\omega_{i}
).

Comparison with lower bounds and uniform sampling

As discussed earlier in Section
3.2
, it is easy to see that the obtained convergence rate for GDA-RR/SO is nearly tight, i.e., differs from the lower bound only by an exponentially decaying term
\exp(-K/5\kappa^{2})
. In fact, Theorem
C.2
implies an
\tilde{O}(1/nK^{2})
convergence rate (suppressing constants such as
\kappa,\mu,\sigma_{*}
as well as logarithmic factors) when
\exp(-K/5\kappa^{2})+1/nK^{2}=O(1/nK^{2})
, which holds when
K
satisfies an epoch requirement of the form
K\geq 10\kappa^{2}\log(n^{1/2}K)
. Thus, as per Theorem
C.2
, GDA-RR/SO improves upon SGDA with replacement and matches the lower bound (modulo logarithmic factors) when
K\geq 10\kappa^{2}\log(n^{1/2}K)
. Furthermore, since our analysis of GDA-RR/SO recovers the rates of full-batch GDA, one can infer that the dependence of this epoch requirement on
\kappa
cannot be improved for constant step-sizes (i.e., using constant step-sizes, one cannot obtain
\tilde{O}(1/nK^{2})
rates by imposing an epoch requirement of the form
K\geq C\kappa^{a}\log(n^{1/2}K)
with
a<2
), as it would contradict the optimality of the
-K/5\kappa^{2}
exponential dependence. We remark that it might be possible to relax or even remove this epoch requirement for matching the lower bound, by either assuming strong monotonicity of the components
\omega_{i}
or by using time-varying step-sizes, as was done in
Mishchenko et al. 2020
and
Ahn et al. 2020
respectively, in the minimization setting.

C.3
Analysis of GDA-AS

Theorem C.3

(Convergence of GDA-AS)
.

Consider Problem (
4
) for the
\mu
-strongly monotone operator
\nu(\mathbf{z})=\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\mathbf{z})
where each
\omega_{i}
is
l
-Lipschitz, but not necessarily monotone. Let
\mathbf{z}^{*}
denote the unique root of
\nu
. Then, for any
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
and
K\geq 1
, the iterates of GDA-AS satisfy the following:

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{3}K}{\mu}.

Setting
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
results in the following expected last-iterate convergence guarantee for GDA-AS, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}|^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+24\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\log^{3}(|\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})|K/\mu)}{\mu^{2}K^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}).

Proof.

The iterate-level update rule of GDA-AS can be expressed as

\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}=\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i-1}-\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i-1}),

(C.14)

where
k\in[K]
,
i\in[n]
and
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
denotes an arbitrary permutation chosen by the adversary at the start of epoch
k
. Recall that Steps 1 and 2 of Theorem
C.2
hold for any permutation
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and consequently, they are directly applicable to GDA-AS. As a result, we can conclude that the epoch-level update rule of GDA-AS is the same as that of GDA-RR/SO, and is given by:

\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\mathbf{H}_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}.

(C.15)

Note that
\mathbf{H}_{k}
and
\mathbf{r}_{k}
are as defined in Theorem
C.2
, and are given by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}-\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(j)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}),

where, as before,
\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t
and
\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0})\textrm{d}t
. Furthermore, by Step 2 of Theorem
C.2
,
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\leq 1-n\alpha\mu/2
.

Our analysis now proceeds by controlling the influence of the noise term
\mathbf{r}_{k}
. Recall that this was achieved in our analysis of GDA-RR/SO by using Lemma
B.3
to upper bound
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
. However, the same strategy does not apply to GDA-AS since the permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
are chosen by the adversary as per an arbitrary strategy that is unknown to us. We circumvent this by instead deriving an upper bound for
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}
that holds uniformly for any permutation
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
.

We begin by repeating some initial steps from Step 3 of Theorem
C.2
. Applying
\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)}\right\rvert\leq l
gives us the following:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(t)}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\right\rvert|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\leq l(1+\alpha l)^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|

\displaystyle\leq l(1+\frac{\mu}{5nl})^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\leq le^{1/5}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|.

By Young’s inequality, we conclude,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{i}|\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}=l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathbf{\omega}_{j}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle=l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)(n\sigma^{2}_{*})\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\leq 3l^{2}n^{4}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4.

We note that the above sequence of inequalities holds for any permutation
\tau_{k}
, and hence, the upper bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 3l^{2}n^{4}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4
holds for any
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n},\ k\in[K]
.

Having bounded both
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert
, we now proceed to derive the last-iterate convergence guarantee.

Unrolling (
C.15
) for
K
epochs and setting
\mathbf{z}^{1}_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{0}
gives us the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{H}_{k}\right](\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k+1}^{K}\mathbf{H}_{k}\right]\mathbf{r}_{k}.

By the triangle inequality and the bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\leq 1-n\alpha\mu/2
, we infer,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k+1}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{k}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K-k}\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert.

By applying Young’s inequality, we can conclude that,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+(1.5l^{2}n^{4}\sigma^{2}_{*})\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3l^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\alpha^{3}n^{3}K}{\mu}.

Since the above inequality holds for any sequence of permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
chosen by the adversary, we obtain the following uniform guarantee for GDA-AS, whenever
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{3}K}{\mu}.

(C.16)

To complete the proof, we substitute
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
in (
C.16
) and proceed in a manner similar to Theorem
C.2
.

Under this choice of step-size, using
\alpha\leq\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}
, we bound the second term in the right hand side of (
C.16
) as

\displaystyle\frac{3l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{3}K}{2\mu}\leq\frac{24l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\mu^{4}}\frac{\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{K^{2}}.

Substituting the above inequality into (
C.16
), we obtain,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{24\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{K^{2}}.

(C.17)

We now consider the following cases:

Case 1:
\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}\leq\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}

By our choice of the step-size,
\alpha=\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
. It follows that:

\displaystyle 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}=2e^{-K/(5\kappa^{2})}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

Substituting into (
C.17
), we obtain the following rate for Case 1,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{\frac{-K}{5\kappa^{2}}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{24\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{K^{2}}.

(C.18)

Case 2:
\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}

By our choice of the step-size,
\alpha=\frac{2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}{\mu nK}
. It follows that:

\displaystyle 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\log\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu\right)}\leq\frac{\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}}\frac{2}{K^{2}}\leq\frac{2}{K^{2}},

where the last inequality follows from the
\mu
-strong monotonicity of
\nu
.

Substituting the above inequality into (
C.17
), we obtain the following rate for Case 2,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{2\mu^{2}+24\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\log^{3}(|\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})|K/\mu)}{\mu^{2}K^{2}}.

(C.19)

By taking the maximum of the right hand side of (
C.18
) and (
C.19
), we finally obtain the desired last-iterate convergence rate.

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}|^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+24\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\log^{3}(|\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})|K/\mu)}{\mu^{2}K^{2}}.

(C.20)

Suppressing constant terms and logarithmic factors, we get,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}).

(C.21)

∎

Comparison with lower bounds

By repeating the same arguments as those used for GDA-RR/SO, it is easy to see that our analysis of GDA-AS recovers the tight rates of full-batch GDA in the deterministic, i.e.,
n=1
regime. Furthermore, Theorem
C.2
implies an
\tilde{O}(1/K^{2})
convergence rate, which matches the lower bound modulo logarithmic factors, when
e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}=O(1/K^{2})
, i.e., when
K
satisfies an epoch requirement of the form
K\geq 10\kappa^{2}\log(K)
. As before, since our analysis of GDA-AS recovers the rates of full-batch GDA, the
\kappa^{2}
dependence of the epoch requirement cannot be improved for constant step-sizes.

Appendix D
Analysis of PPM without Replacement

We now present the convergence proofs of the proximal point method without replacement (i.e. PPM-RR/SO/AS). The structure of these results largely mirror that of our analysis of GDA without replacement, since the key techniques used in our analysis is an adaptation of the linearization technique to PPM and the application of Lemma
B.3
to control the influence of noise in PPM without replacement. To this end, we begin by presenting a linearization-based proof of convergence of full batch PPM, followed by a unified proof of convergence of PPM-RR and PPM-SO, and conclude with the extension of our proof techniques to the adversarial shuffling regime by analyzing PPM-AS.

D.1
Analysis of Full-batch PPM by Linearization

Theorem D.1

(Convergence of full-batch PPM)
.

Consider the
l
-Lipschitz and
\mu
-strongly monotone operator
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\mathbf{z}^{*}
denote the unique root of
\nu
. For any step-size
0<\alpha<1/l
, the iterates
\mathbf{z}_{k}
of full-batch PPM satisfy the following recurrence:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})^{-1}(\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*}),

where
\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1}
is invertible and its singular values are lower bounded as
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})\geq 1+\alpha\mu
. Consequently, setting
\alpha=1/2l
gives us the following last iterate convergence guarantee:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{K+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq(1+1/2\kappa)^{-2K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

Proof.

The iterates of full-batch PPM with step-size
\alpha
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k+1}=\mathbf{z}_{k}-\alpha\nu(\mathbf{z}_{k+1}).

From the above equation, we conclude that
\mathbf{z}_{k+1}
is a fixed point of the operator
\zeta(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}_{k}-\alpha\nu(\mathbf{z})
. Since
\alpha<1/l
,
\zeta
is a contraction mapping. Hence, by the Banach Fixed Point Theorem, we conclude that
\mathbf{z}_{k+1}
exists and is uniquely defined. This allows us to proceed in a manner similar to Theorem
C.1
and linearize
\nu(\mathbf{z}_{k+1})
about
\mathbf{z}^{*}
. Thus, by the Lipschitz continuity of
\nu
and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus for Lebesgue Integrals:

\displaystyle\nu(\mathbf{z}_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\nu(\mathbf{z}^{*})+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\nu(t\mathbf{z}_{k+1}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})(\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}_{k+1}(\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}),

where
\mathbf{M}_{k+1}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\nu(t\mathbf{z}_{k+1}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t
is well defined by Lemma
B.1
. Substituting this expansion into the PPM update rule gives us the following:

\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})(\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}).

(D.1)

Using the same arguments as in Theorem
C.1
, we conclude that the
\mu
-strong monotonicity of
\nu
implies
\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}_{k+1}\mathbf{v}\geq\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}\ \forall\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using this, we lower bound the singular values of
\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1}
as follows:

Note that for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}_{k+1}\mathbf{v}+\alpha\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}^{T}_{k+1}\mathbf{v}+\alpha^{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{k+1}\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1+2\alpha\mu+\alpha^{2}\mu^{2})\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2},

where the last inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality since
\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{k+1}\mathbf{v}\right\rvert\geq\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}_{k+1}\mathbf{v}\geq\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}
.

Thus, the singular values of
\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1}
are lower bounded as
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})\geq 1+\alpha\mu>0
. Since
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{M}_{k+1}
is a square matrix, this implies that
\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1}
is invertible and

\displaystyle\left\lvert(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})^{-1}\right\rvert=\sigma_{\max}((\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})^{-1})=1/\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})\leq(1+\alpha\mu)^{-1}.

It follows that,

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{k+1})^{-1}(\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle\implies\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\alpha\mu)^{-2}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

Thus, by setting
\alpha=1/2l
, we obtain the following convergence guarantee

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{K+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq(1+1/2\kappa)^{-2K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

∎

D.2
A Unified Analysis of PPM-RR and PPM-SO

Theorem D.2

(Convergence of PPM-RR/SO)
.

Consider Problem (
4
) for the
\mu
-strongly monotone operator
\nu(\mathbf{z})=\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\mathbf{z})
where each
\omega_{i}
is
l
-Lipschitz, but not necessarily monotone. Let
\mathbf{z}^{*}
denote the unique root of
\nu
. Then, for any
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
and
K\geq 1
, the iterates of PPM-RR and PPM-SO satisfy the following:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{2}K}{\mu}.

Setting
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
results in the following expected last-iterate convergence guarantee for both PPM-RR and PPM-SO, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}|^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+8\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\log^{3}(|\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})|n^{1/2}K/\mu)}{\mu^{2}nK^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}}).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we express the iterate-level update rule of PPM-RR and PPM-SO jointly as

\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}=\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i-1}-\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}),

(D.2)

where
k\in[K]
and
i\in[n]
. Similar to Theorem
D.1
, the Banach Fixed Point Theorem guarantees that
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}
is well defined and unique since
\alpha\leq\mu/5nl^{2}<1/l
.

We note that
\tau_{k}\sim\textrm{Uniform}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
for every
k\in[K]
for PPM-RR (i.e.,
\tau_{k}
is a permutation of
[n]
that is resampled uniformly at every epoch) whereas for PPM-SO,
\tau_{k}=\tau\ \forall k\in[K]
where
\tau\sim\textrm{Uniform}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
(i.e., the permutation
\tau
is uniformly sampled before the first epoch, then reused for all subsequent epochs). We also define the transposed permutation
\tilde{\tau}_{k}
as
\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i)=\tau_{k}(n+1-i)
for any
i\in[n]
.

The remainder of our proof follows the structure of Theorem
C.2
. In particular, we apply the linearization technique as illustrated in Theorem
D.1
to PPM without replacement and derive a linearized epoch-level update rule of the form
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\mathbf{H}_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}
. This is followed by lower bounding
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{H}_{k})
using the Lipschitz continuity and strong monotonicity of
\mu
and upper bounding
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
using Lemma
B.3
. The proof is completed by unrolling the update rule for
K
epochs and carefully choosing the step-size.

Step 1: Linearized epoch-level update rule

The approach for this step mirrors that of Theorem
C.2
and hinges on the insight that for small enough step-sizes, the dynamics of the iterates
\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}
of PPM without replacement can be treated as a noisy version of full-batch PPM. Complementing this insight with the linearization-based analysis of full-batch PPM in Theorem
D.1
motivates the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+[\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})-\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})]+[\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i})-\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}-\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})\textrm{d}t.

We further define
\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})\textrm{d}t.

By repeating the same arguments as in Theorem
D.1
, we note that the
l
-Lipschitz continuity of
\omega_{i}(\mathbf{z})\ \forall i\in[n]
implies that
\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}
are well defined and bounded as
\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l
and
\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l
. It follows that

\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i})=\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}-\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}).

(D.3)

Substituting (
D.3
) into the update equation (
C.2
) for
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}
yields,

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(n)})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\sigma_{k}(n)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(n)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}).

Repeating the same for
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}
results in the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-2}-\mathbf{z}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(n-1)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(n-1)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(n-1)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}-\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}-\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n-1}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha(\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}-\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})[(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})]

\displaystyle+\alpha(\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}-\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle=[(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)})+\alpha(\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}-\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})](\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle+\alpha[\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})]

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}+\alpha(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)}](\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle+\alpha[\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})+(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(2)})\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})].

By applying the same process for preceeding iterates and substituting
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}=\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}
, we obtain the following epoch-level update rule for PPM-RR and PPM-SO

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{I}+\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}](\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle+\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}).

(D.4)

We clarify that the matrix products in (
D.4
) are in reverse order, and hence reduce to the empty product, which is defined to be
\mathbf{I}
, when
j=i
. Furthermore
\tilde{\tau}_{k}
denotes the transposed permutation
\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i)=\tau_{k}(n+1-i)
.

We simplify the second term in the right hand side of (
D.4
) using the summation by parts identity. To this end, we define
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
as

\displaystyle a_{j}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)}),

\displaystyle b_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}),

and observe that,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})=n\nu(\mathbf{z}^{*})=0,

since
\mathbf{z}^{*}
is the unique root of
\nu
. We now apply the summation by parts identity to obtain the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}b_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(a_{i+1}-a_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{i}b_{j}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})-\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right]\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle=\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}).

Hence, we conclude that:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})=\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}).

We define
\mathbf{H}_{k}
and
\mathbf{r}_{k}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}+\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}),

and substitute the above expressions in (
D.4
). This gives us the following noisy linearized update rule for the epoch iterates
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}
of PPM-RR and PPM-SO:

\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\mathbf{H}_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}.

(D.5)

We observe that (
D.5
) closely resembles the linearized update rule (
D.1
) of full-batch PPM as derived in Theorem
D.1
with an additive noise term
\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}
. In fact, for
n=1
, which corresponds to the full-batch regime, it is easy to see that
\mathbf{r}_{k}=0
and (
D.5
) reduces to (
D.1
). As we shall see in Theorem
D.3
, the same update rule also applies to PPM-AS. Similar to our analysis of GDA, the derivation of an epoch-level update rule that simultaneously handles PPM-RR, PPM-SO and PPM-AS is a key component of our analysis.

Thus, we proceed in a manner similar to Theorem
D.1
by lower bounding the minimum singular value of
\mathbf{H}_{k}
.

Step 2: Lower bounding
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{H}_{k})

We define the matrix
\mathbf{M}
as,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle=1/n\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}=1/n\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\nu(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{n}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t.

By following the same arguments as Theorem
D.1
, we note that the
l
-smoothness and
\mu
-strong monotonicity of
\nu
implies that
\left\lvert\mathbf{M}\right\rvert\leq l
and
\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}\geq\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}\ \forall\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

By expanding the product terms in
\mathbf{H}_{k}
, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}+\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}+n\alpha\mathbf{M}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n}\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{j})}\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{j-1})}\ldots\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{2})}\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{1})}.

We now apply Weyl’s inequality for singular value perturbations, which gives us the following bound,

\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{H}_{k})\geq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{I}+n\alpha\mathbf{M})-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n}\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{j})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{j-1})}\right\rvert\ldots\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{2})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{1})}\right\rvert.

(D.6)

Following the same steps as Theorem
D.1
, we conclude that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}\geq\mu\left\lvert\mathbf{v}\right\rvert^{2}
implies
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{I}+n\alpha\mathbf{M})\geq 1+n\alpha\mu
. Furthermore, we observe that,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\sum_{1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{j}\leq n}\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{j})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{j-1})}\right\rvert\ldots\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{2})}\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t_{1})}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha^{j}\binom{n}{j}l^{j}.

Substituting the above bound in (
D.6
) gives us the following:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{H}_{k})

\displaystyle\geq 1+n\alpha\mu-\sum_{j=2}^{n}(\alpha l)^{j}\binom{n}{j}\geq 1+n\alpha\mu-\sum_{j=2}^{n}(\alpha nl)^{j}\geq 1+n\alpha\mu-\frac{\alpha^{2}n^{2}l^{2}}{1-\alpha nl}

\displaystyle\geq 1+n\alpha\mu-\frac{5\alpha^{2}n^{2}l^{2}}{4}\geq 1+3n\alpha\mu/4,

where we substitute
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}\leq 1/5
wherever required. Furthermore, since
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{H}_{k})\geq 1+3n\alpha\mu/4>0
,
\mathbf{H}_{k}
is invertible and
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right\rvert\leq(1+3n\alpha\mu/4)^{-1}\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)
, which holds since
n\alpha\mu\leq 1/5
.

As before, our analysis so far holds for any permutation
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Consequently, Steps 1 and 2 directly generalize to the adversarial shuffling regime without any modifications. We use this as a starting point for our analysis of PPM-AS in Theorem
D.3
.

We now control the magnitude of the noise term
\mathbf{r}_{k}
by bounding
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
similar to Theorem
C.2
. We note that the expectation is taken over the single uniform permutation
\tau
for PPM-SO and over independent uniform permutations
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{K}
for PPM-RR.

Step 3: Upper bounding
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

We follow the same procedure as in Step 3 of Theorem
C.2
and obtain the following bound by successively applying
\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l
and Young’s inequality:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}.

We now convert the above inequality into an upper bound for
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
by applying Lemma
B.3
to the term
|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}
. The case of PPM-RR and PPM-SO are discussed separately.

Step 3A:
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
for PPM-RR

We write
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
to explicitly denote the dependence of the expectation on the uniformly sampled random permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
, and then proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{k}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}].

Since
\tau_{k}
is a uniformly sampled random permutation and
\mathbb{S}_{n}
is a finite group with
n!
elements, it follows that for any
\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\textrm{Uniform}}\left[\tau_{k}=\pi\right]=\mathbb{P}_{\textrm{Uniform}}\left[\tilde{\tau}_{k}=\pi\right]=1/n!\ .

Hence, we infer that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{k}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]=\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\sigma_{*}^{2},

where the last equality follows from Lemma
B.3
. Note that
\sigma_{*}^{2}
denotes the gradient variance at the minimax point
\mathbf{z}^{*}
, defined as
\sigma_{*}^{2}=1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{\omega}_{i}(\mathbf{z}^{*})\right\rvert^{2}
. Substituting the above into the upper bound for
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
yields,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{k}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\sigma_{*}^{2}=l^{2}e^{2/5}\sigma_{*}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i(n-i)

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4.

Step 3B:
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
for PPM-SO

For PPM-SO,
\tau_{1}=\ldots=\tau_{K}=\tau
where
\tau
is a uniformly sampled random permutation. Consequently,
\tilde{\tau}_{1}=\ldots=\tilde{\tau}_{K}=\tilde{\tau}
where
\tilde{\tau}
is the transposed permutation of
\tau
. Hence, we conclude that,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}.

Proceeding along the same lines as Step 3A, we use the fact that
\tau
is a uniformly sampled random permutation to obtain the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]

\displaystyle=l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\tau}}[|1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\sigma_{*}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4.

Hence, for both PPM-RR and PPM-SO,
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert
is bounded in expectation as
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4
.

Step 4: Convergence analysis

Since
\mathbf{H}_{k}
is invertible, we write (
D.5
) as

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}[\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}-\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}].

Unrolling for
K
epochs and setting
\mathbf{z}^{1}_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{0}
yields,

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right](\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})-\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k}^{K}\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{j}\right]\mathbf{r}_{k}.

By the triangle inequality and the bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right\rvert\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{j}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K-k}\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert,

and as a result of Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}.

By taking expectations (with respect to the uniform random permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
for RR and
\tau
for SO respectively) on both sides of the above inequality and substituting the upper bound for
\mathbb{E}[|\mathbf{r}_{k}|{}^{2}]
for PPM-RR and PPM-SO as derived in Step 3, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+0.5l^{2}n^{3}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}.

This yields the following guarantee that holds for PPM-RR and PPM-SO whenever
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
,

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{2}K}{\mu}.

(D.7)

We note that the above inequality is identical to the inequality (
C.8
) obtained in Theorem
C.2
. Hence, we substitute
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
in (
D.7
) and follow the same steps as in Theorem
C.2
to obtain the following last-iterate convergence guarantee for both PPM-RR and PPM-SO, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq 2e^{-\frac{K}{5\kappa^{2}}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+8\kappa^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\log^{3}\left(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert n^{1/2}K/\mu\right)}{\mu^{2}nK^{2}}.

(D.8)

Suppressing constant terms and logarithmic factors, we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}]=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}}).

(D.9)

∎

D.3
Analysis of PPM-AS

Theorem D.3

(Convergence of PPM-AS)
.

Consider Problem (
4
) for the
\mu
-strongly monotone operator
\nu(\mathbf{z})=\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\mathbf{z})
where each
\omega_{i}
is
l
-Lipschitz, but not necessarily monotone. Let
\mathbf{z}^{*}
denote the unique root of
\nu
. Then, for any
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
and
K\geq 1
, the iterates of PPM-AS satisfy the following:

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{3}K}{\mu}.

Setting
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
results in the following last-iterate convergence guarantee for PPM-AS, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}|^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+24\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\log^{3}(|\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})|K/\mu)}{\mu^{2}K^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}).

Proof.

The iterate-level update rule of PPM-AS can be expressed as

\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}=\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i-1}-\alpha\mathbf{\omega}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}),

(D.10)

where
k\in[K]
,
i\in[n]
and
\tau_{k}
denotes an arbitrary permutation of
[n]
which is chosen by the adversary at the start of epoch
k
. For any permutation
\tau_{k}
chosen by the adversary, we denote the transposed permutation
\tilde{\tau}_{k}
as
\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i)=\tau_{k}(n+1-i)
. Furthermore
\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}
is well defined and unique due to the Banach Fixed Point Theorem as
\alpha\leq\mu/5nl^{2}<1/l
.

We recall that Steps 1 and 2 of Theorem
D.2
are applicable for any permutation
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and hence, can be applied directly to PPM-AS. Consequently, the epoch-level update rule of PPM-AS is the same as that of PPM-RR/SO and is given by:

\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\mathbf{H}_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k},

(D.11)

where
\mathbf{H}_{k}
and
\mathbf{r}_{k}
are as defined in Theorem
D.2
, and are given by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}+\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right)\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\alpha\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*}),

where, as before,
\mathbf{M}_{\tau_{k}(i)}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{*})\textrm{d}t
and
\mathbf{J}_{\tau_{k}(i)}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\omega_{\tau_{k}(i)}(t\mathbf{z}^{k}_{i}+(1-t)\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})\textrm{d}t
. Furthermore, by Step 2 of Theorem
D.2
,
\mathbf{H}_{k}
is invertible and
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right\rvert\leq 1-n\alpha\mu/2
.

We now proceed to control the noise term
\mathbf{r}_{k}
by deriving an upper bound for
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}
that holds uniformly over all permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
. This is done by following the same steps as that of Theorem
C.3
, which gives us the following bound by successive applications of
\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l
and Young’s inequality:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{i}|\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathbf{\omega}_{\tilde{\tau}_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}=l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathbf{\omega}_{j}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|^{2}

\displaystyle=l^{2}e^{2/5}(n-1)(n\sigma^{2}_{*})\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\leq 3l^{2}n^{4}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4.

We note that the above sequence of inequalities hold for any permutation
\tau_{k}
, and hence, the upper bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 3l^{2}n^{4}\sigma_{*}^{2}/4
holds for any
\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n},\ k\in[K]
.

Since
\mathbf{H}_{k}
is invertible, we write (
D.11
) as

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}[\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}-\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}].

By unrolling for
K
epochs and setting
\mathbf{z}^{1}_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{0}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}=\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right](\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*})-\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k}^{K}\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{j}\right]\mathbf{r}_{k}.

By using the triangle inequality and the bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right\rvert\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)
, we get:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{k=1}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{k}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{j=k}^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{j}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert+\alpha^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{K-k}\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert.

Applying Young’s inequality yields the following:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+2\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}|\mathbf{r}_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+(1.5l^{2}n^{4}\sigma^{2}_{*})\alpha^{4}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-n\alpha\mu/2)^{2(K-k)}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3l^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}n^{3}\alpha^{3}K}{\mu}.

Since the above inequality holds for any sequence of permutations
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}
chosen by the adversary, we obtain the following uniform guarantee for GDA-AS whenever
\alpha\leq\frac{\mu}{5nl^{2}}
,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{-n\alpha\mu K}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3l^{2}\sigma_{*}^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{3}K}{\mu}.

(D.12)

We note that the above inequality is exactly identical to the inequality (
C.16
) obtained in Theorem
C.3
. Hence, we substitute
\alpha=\min\{\mu/5nl^{2},2\log(\left\lvert\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right\rvert K/\mu)/\mu nK\}
in (
D.12
) and follow the same steps as in Theorem
C.3
to obtain the following last-iterate convergence guarantee for PPM-AS, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}|^{2}+\frac{2\mu^{2}+24\kappa^{2}\sigma^{2}_{*}\log^{3}(|\nu(\mathbf{z}_{0})|K/\mu)}{\mu^{2}K^{2}}.

(D.13)

Suppressing constant terms and logarithmic factors, we get,

\max_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{5\kappa^{2}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}).

(D.14)

∎

Appendix E
Analysis of AGDA-RR and AGDA-AS

In this section, we present the convergence analysis of AGDA-RR and AGDA-AS for smooth finite-sum objectives that satisfy a two-sided PŁ inequality. We begin by stating some useful properties of two-sided PŁ functions which have been established in prior works and are crucial to our analysis. We then present our convergence analysis of AGDA-RR and AGDA-AS, which follows the same broad structure as our analysis of GDA-RR/AS.

E.1
Properties of Two-sided PŁ Functions

We begin by presenting three important properties of two-sided PŁ functions that are used throughout our analysis of AGDA-RR/AS

Lemma E.1

(Equivalent optimality conditions for two-sided PŁ functions)
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfy Assumption
3
and let
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})
be any point in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})
is a global minimax point, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}}\Phi(\mathbf{x})
where
\Phi
is the best response function defined by
\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, and
\mathbf{y}^{*}\in\arg\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y})
.

•

(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})
is a saddle point, i.e.,

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y})\leq F(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})\leq F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{*}),\ \forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}.

•

(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})
is a stationary point, i.e.,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})=\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})=0.

Proof.

See Lemma 2.1 of
Yang et al. 2020
.
∎

Lemma E.2

(Properties of the best response)
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an
l
-smooth function satisfying Assumption
3
(with constants
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
) and let
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the best response function defined as
\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Then
\Phi
satisfies the following properties:

•

\Phi
is differentiable and
L
-smooth where
L=l+l^{2}/\mu_{2}
.

•

\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
where
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
is any arbitrary point in
\arg\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

•

\Phi
satisfies the (one-sided) PŁ inequality with constant
\mu_{1}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}\geq 2\mu_{1}[\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi^{*}],

where
\Phi^{*}=\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}}\Phi(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

See Lemma A.2 and Lemma A.3 of
Yang et al. 2020
.
∎

Lemma E.3

(Quadratic growth properties of PŁ functions)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a PŁ function, i.e., there exists a positive constant
\mu
such that
\left\lvert\nabla f(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}\geq 2\mu[f(\mathbf{x})-f^{*}]
, where
f^{*}=\min_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x})
. Then,
f
satisfies the quadratic growth property with constant
\mu
, i.e., for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
,
f(\mathbf{x})-f^{*}\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{x}\right\rvert^{2}
where
\mathbf{x}^{*}
is the projection of
\mathbf{x}
on the set
\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

See Lemma A.1 of
Yang et al. 2020
.
∎

Finally, we motivate the necessity of the bounded gradient variance assumption by presenting the construction of a two-sided PŁ function whose set of global minimax points is unbounded.

Lemma E.4

(2PŁ function with unbounded set of global minimax points)
.

Consider the function
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2})^{2}/2-(\mathbf{y}_{1}+\mathbf{y}_{2})^{2}/2
. Then,
f
is a two-sided PŁ function with constants
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=2
. Furthermore, the set of global minimax points of
F
is an unbounded proper subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

Proof.

We define the functions
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{(\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2})^{2}}{2},

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=-\frac{(\mathbf{y}_{1}+\mathbf{y}_{2})^{2}}{2}.

Furthermore, we note that:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=-[\mathbf{y}_{1}+\mathbf{y}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{1}+\mathbf{y}_{2}].

From the above inequalities, it follows that:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=4[f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\Psi(\mathbf{y})]=4[f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})],

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=4[\Phi(\mathbf{x})-f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]=4[\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})].

Thus,
f
satisfies the two-sided PŁ inequality with constants
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=2
. Consequently, Lemma
E.1
implies that the set of minimax points are exactly the ones satisfying
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
, i.e., the set of global minimax points is
\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\ |\ \mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2}=0,\mathbf{y}_{1}+\mathbf{y}_{2}=0\}
, which is an unbounded proper subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
∎

E.2
Analysis of AGDA-RR

Theorem E.1

(Convergence of AGDA-RR)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
3
, and
4
be satisfied and let
\eta=\nicefrac{{73l^{2}}}{{2\mu^{2}_{2}}}
. Then, for any
\alpha\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{5\eta nl}}
,
\beta=\eta\alpha
, and
K\geq 1
, the iterates of AGDA-RR satisfy the following:

\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+0.42n\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}.

Setting
\alpha=\min\{1/5\eta nl,4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/\mu_{1}nK\}
results in the following expected last iterate convergence guarantee, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{\mu_{1}+c\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{\mu_{1}nK^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}}),

where
\kappa=\max\{\nicefrac{{l}}{{\mu_{1}}},\nicefrac{{l}}{{\mu_{2}}}\}
and
c>0
is a numerical constant that is independent of
l,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and
\sigma^{2}
.

Proof.

Before presenting the convergence analysis of AGDA-RR, we introduce some additional notation. For any
k\in[K]
and any random variable
Y
, we use
\mathbb{E}_{k}[Y]
to denote the expectation of
Y
conditioned on all previous epoch iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{j}_{0})_{j=1}^{k}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{k}[Y]=\mathbb{E}[Y\ |\ \mathbf{x}^{1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{1}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}]
. Similarly, for any two random variables
Y,Z
, we use
\mathbb{E}_{k}[Y\ |\ Z]
to denote the expectation of
Y
conditioned on all previous epoch iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{j}_{0})_{j=1}^{k}
and
Z
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}_{k}[Y\ |\ Z]=\mathbb{E}[Y\ |\ \mathbf{x}^{1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{1}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0},Z]
.

Our analysis follows a structure similar to that of GDA-RR/SO and consists of three major steps: 1. obtaining an epoch level update rule for AGDA-RR that resembles the update of deterministic AGDA with noise, 2. bounding the noise in the update rule using Lemma
B.3
, 3. presenting a convergence analysis with respect to the Lyapunov function
V_{\lambda}
.

Step 1: Epoch level update rule

We begin with the observation that the
\mathbf{x}
-iterates of AGDA-RR are the same as that of gradient descent without replacement on
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})
and thus, can be treated as a noisy version of full-batch gradient descent. To this end, we linearize the stochastic gradients
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})
about
(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})
and obtain the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\left[\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}+t(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\textrm{d}t\right](\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),

where
\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i)}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}+t(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\textrm{d}t
. As before, by Rademacher’s Theorem, the
l
-smoothness of
f_{\tau_{k}(i)}
guarantees that
\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i)}
is well defined and satisfies
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l
.

When substituting the above linearization into the
\mathbf{x}
-iterate updates, we observe the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{k}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{k}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{1}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{1},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{1}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{1}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(2)}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}-\alpha(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(2)})\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(1)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(2)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

Repeating the above steps for subsequent iterates
\mathbf{x}^{k}_{3},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{k}_{n}
, we obtain the following epoch-level update rule for
\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{n}
:

\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}-\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right)\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

(E.1)

We further simplify (
E.1
) using the summation by parts identity. To this end, we define
c_{j}
and
e_{j}
as follows:

\displaystyle c_{j}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)}),

\displaystyle e_{j}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

We note that
c_{n}=\mathbf{I}
, since
\widetilde{\prod}_{t=n+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})
denotes the empty reverse product. Hence, we conclude,

\displaystyle c_{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})=n\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{i}e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})-\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+1}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right]\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle=\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

We define
\mathbf{r}_{k}
as,

\mathbf{r}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

(E.2)

From the summation by parts identity
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}e_{i}=c_{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{i}e_{j}
, we obtain the following:

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}[\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)}]\right]\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})=n\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\alpha\mathbf{r}_{k}.

By substituting the above equation into (
E.1
), we obtain the following update rule for the epoch iterates
\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}
:

\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}-n\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}.

(E.3)

The epoch-level update rule for the
\mathbf{y}
-iterate is derived in a similar fashion. We begin by linearizing
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{i-1})
as:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{i-1})=\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{y}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),

where
\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i)}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}+t(\mathbf{y}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}))\textrm{d}t
satisfies
\left\lvert\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i)}\right\rvert\leq l
. As before, by substituting into the
\mathbf{y}
iterate updates and unrolling, we obtain,

\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}+\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=j+1}^{n}[\mathbf{I}+\beta\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(t)}]\right]\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

(E.4)

Once again, applying the summation by parts technique gives us the following update rule for the epoch iterates
\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}
:
