\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}+n\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\beta^{2}\mathbf{s}_{k},

(E.5)

where
\mathbf{s}_{k}
is given by,

\mathbf{s}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\beta\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

(E.6)

Step 2: Controlling the noise terms

By applying the triangle inequality on (
E.2
) and substituting
\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}}(i)\right\rvert\leq l
and
\alpha\leq 1/5nl
wherever required, we bound
\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert
as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)}\right\rvert\right]\left\lvert\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\right\rvert|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq l(1+\alpha l)^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|\leq le^{1/5}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq le^{1/5}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq le^{1/5}\left[n^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert/2+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|\right].

By defining
d_{k,i}=|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|
and substituting into the above inequality, we obtain,

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert\leq le^{1/5}\left[n^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert/2+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}id_{k,i}\right].

(E.7)

By applying Young’s inequality to (
E.7
), we conclude:

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2l^{2}e^{2/5}\left[n^{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}/4+(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}d^{2}_{k,i}\right].

(E.8)

By Lemma
B.3
and the bounded gradient variance assumption, we bound
\mathbb{E}[d^{2}_{k,i}]
as follows:

\mathbb{E}[d^{2}_{k,i}]=\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\left[1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}\sigma^{2},

(E.9)

where the expectation is taken over the uniformly sampled random permutation
\tau_{k}
. Furthermore, applying Jensen’s inequality to (
E.9
) gives us the following bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[d_{k,i}]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[d^{2}_{k,i}]}\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{n-i}{i(n-1)}}.

(E.10)

From (
E.10
), it follows that,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\mathbb{E}[d_{k,i}]

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sqrt{i}\sqrt{\frac{n-i}{n-1}}\leq\sigma\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sqrt{i}\leq\frac{2\sigma n^{3/2}}{3}.

By taking expectations of (
E.7
) and substituting the above, we get,

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert]\leq\frac{2n^{2}l}{3}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert+\sigma ln^{3/2}.

(E.11)

Similarly, from (
E.9
), it follows that,

\displaystyle(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}\mathbb{E}[d_{k,i}^{2}]\leq\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i(n-i)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}n^{3}}{6}.

By taking expectations of (
E.8
) and substituting the above, we obtain,

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq\frac{3n^{4}l^{2}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}l^{2}n^{3}}{2}.

(E.12)

We bound
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert]
and
\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]
by following a similar strategy and obtain:

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert]\leq\frac{2n^{2}l}{3}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert+\sigma ln^{3/2},

(E.13)

\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]\leq\frac{3n^{4}l^{2}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}l^{2}n^{3}}{2}.

(E.14)

Step 3: Obtaining a convergence guarantee
We begin by defining
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
as:

\displaystyle a_{k}

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-\Phi^{*},

\displaystyle b_{k}

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

By definition of
V_{\lambda}
, we conclude that
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0})=a_{k}+\lambda b_{k}
. Furthermore, we note that by Lemma
E.2
,
\Phi
is
L
-smooth where
L=l+l^{2}/\mu_{2}
. Since
\alpha\leq 1/5\eta nl
, it follows that
n\alpha L\leq 1
. Using these properties, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),-n\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\left\lvert-n\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-n\alpha\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\frac{\alpha}{n}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}n^{2}\alpha^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\frac{\alpha}{n}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-\frac{\alpha}{n}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{3}}{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}.

By taking expectations conditioned on the epoch iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{j}_{0})_{j=1}^{k}
and substituting the bound (
E.12
), we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})]-\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3n^{3}\alpha^{3}l^{2}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

(E.15)

We proceed by applying the
l
-smoothness of
-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\mathbf{x}
, to obtain the following:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq-\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}\right\rangle+\frac{l}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),n\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{l}{2}\left\lvert n\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(n\alpha+\frac{l}{2}n^{2}\alpha^{2})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{l}{2}\left\lvert\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle-(\alpha^{2}+ln\alpha^{3})\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq(n\alpha+\frac{l}{2}n^{2}\alpha^{2})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{l\alpha^{4}}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{6\alpha^{2}}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert.

(E.16)

From (
E.11
) and (
E.12
), we infer that:

\displaystyle\frac{l\alpha^{4}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3n^{4}\alpha^{4}l^{3}}{8}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}l^{3}\alpha^{4}n^{3}}{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3n^{4}\alpha^{4}l^{3}}{8}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}l^{2}\alpha^{3}n^{2}}{20},

\displaystyle\frac{6\alpha^{2}}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6\alpha^{2}n^{\frac{3}{2}}\sigma l}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert+\frac{4n^{2}\alpha^{2}l}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=(\frac{6\alpha^{\frac{3}{2}}nl\sigma}{5})(\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(x_{0}^{k},y_{0}^{k})\right\rvert)+\frac{4n^{2}\alpha^{2}l}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{n\alpha}{2}+\frac{4n^{2}\alpha^{2}l}{5})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{18\alpha^{3}n^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{25}.

Taking expectations on both sides of (
E.16
) by conditioning on the epoch iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{j}_{0})_{j=1}^{k}
and substituting the above inequalities, we obtain:

F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{k}[F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})]\leq(\frac{3n\alpha}{2}+\frac{13}{10}ln^{2}\alpha^{2}+\frac{3n^{4}\alpha^{4}l^{3}}{8})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{4\alpha^{3}n^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{5}.

(E.17)

We now apply the
l
-smoothness of
-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\mathbf{y}
to obtain the following:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq-\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}\right\rangle+\frac{l}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0}-\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),n\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\beta^{2}\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{l}{2}\left\lvert n\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\beta^{2}\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(-n\beta+ln^{2}\beta^{2})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\beta^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert+l\beta^{4}\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}.

(E.18)

From (
E.13
) and (
E.14
), we conclude,

\displaystyle l\beta^{4}\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3n^{4}l^{3}\beta^{4}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}l^{3}n^{3}\beta^{4}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3n^{4}l^{3}\beta^{4}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}l^{2}n^{2}\beta^{3}}{10},

\displaystyle\beta^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert]

\displaystyle\leq\sigma ln^{3/2}\beta^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert+\frac{2n^{2}\beta^{2}l}{3}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{n^{\frac{1}{2}}\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert)(2n\beta^{\frac{3}{2}}l\sigma)+\frac{2n^{2}\beta^{2}l}{3}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{n\beta}{8}+\frac{2n^{2}\beta^{2}l}{3})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+2n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}.

We proceed by taking expectations on both sides of (
E.18
) by conditioning on the epoch iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{j}_{0})_{j=1}^{k}
and
\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}
and substituting the above bounds. Consequently, we obtain the following inequality:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{k}[F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0})|\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq(-\frac{7n\beta}{8}+\frac{5n^{2}\beta^{2}l}{3}+\frac{3n^{4}\beta^{4}l^{3}}{4})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{21n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{n\beta}{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{21n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

The two-sided PŁ condition on
F
implies that,

\displaystyle-\frac{n\beta}{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-n\beta\mu_{2}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})],

which gives us the following:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0})|\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}]\leq(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})]+\frac{21n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

Taking expectations of the above with respect to
\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}
and applying the law of iterated expectation, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})]+\frac{21n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

(E.19)

From (
E.2
), we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[a_{k+1}]\leq a_{k}+n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\\
+\frac{3n\alpha}{100}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

(E.20)

Furthermore, we note that,

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})=b_{k}+F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}).

By substituting the above into (
E.19
), we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[b_{k+1}]\leq(1-n\beta\mu_{2})b_{k}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\mathbb{E}_{k}[F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})]\\
+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})]+\frac{21n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

By substituting (
E.17
) and (
E.2
) into the above, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{k}[b_{k+1}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-n\beta\mu_{2})b_{k}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle-(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{9n\alpha}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{13n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}+\frac{21n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

(E.21)

From (
E.20
) and (
E.21
), we get the following by substituting
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})=a_{k+1}+\lambda b_{k+1}
,

\mathbb{E}_{k}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq a_{k}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\lambda b_{k}+[n\alpha+\lambda n\alpha(1-n\beta\mu_{2})]\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+C_{3}\\
-[\frac{n\alpha}{2}+\frac{\lambda n\alpha}{2}(1-n\beta\mu_{2})]\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+[\frac{3n\alpha}{100}+\lambda(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{9n\alpha}{5}]\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2},

(E.22)

where
C_{3}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{3}=[\frac{1}{2}+\frac{13\lambda}{10}(1-n\beta\mu_{2})]n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}+\frac{21\lambda n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

By Young’s inequality, the following holds for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+(1+1/\varepsilon)\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}.

Substituting this into (
E.22
), we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq a_{k}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\lambda b_{k}+C_{3}\\
-[\frac{n\alpha}{2}-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{3n\alpha}{100}-\lambda(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[(1+\varepsilon)\frac{9n\alpha}{5}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}]]\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\\
+[n\alpha+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{3n\alpha}{100}+\lambda(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[n\alpha+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{9n\alpha}{5}]]\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}.

(E.23)

From Lemma
E.2
, we know that
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
satisfies the PŁ inequality with constant
\mu_{1}
. Thus, we conclude that

\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\geq 2\mu_{1}(\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-\Phi^{*})=2\mu_{1}a_{k}.

(E.24)

Let
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})
denote the projection of
\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}
on the set
\arg\max_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y})
. From Lemma
E.2
, we conclude that
\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}))
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}))\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq l^{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

(E.25)

Furthermore, we note that Assumption
3
implies that
-F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y})
satisfies the PŁ inequality with constant
\mu_{2}
. Thus, by Lemma
E.3
, we conclude that,

\displaystyle b_{k}=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\geq\frac{\mu_{2}}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}.

By substituting the above inequality in (
E.25
), we conclude that,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{2l^{2}b_{k}}{\mu_{2}}.

We proceed by substituting the above bound in (
E.23
) to obtain the following upper bound on
\mathbb{E}_{k}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]
:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq\{1-[\mu_{1}n\alpha-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{3\mu_{1}n\alpha}{50}-\lambda(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[(1+\varepsilon)\frac{18\mu_{1}n\alpha}{5}-\mu_{1}n\alpha]]\}a_{k}+C_{3}\\
+[1-n\beta\mu_{2}+\frac{2l^{2}n\alpha}{\mu_{2}\lambda}+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{3l^{2}n\alpha}{50\mu_{2}\lambda}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[\frac{2l^{2}n\alpha}{\mu_{2}}+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{18l^{2}n\alpha}{5\mu_{2}}]]\lambda b_{k}.

Hence, we obtain the following update equation for
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})
:

\mathbb{E}_{k}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq C_{1}a_{k}+C_{2}\lambda b_{k}+C_{3},

(E.26)

where
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
are given by,

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=1-[\mu_{1}n\alpha-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{3\mu_{1}n\alpha}{50}-\lambda(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[(1+\varepsilon)\frac{18\mu_{1}n\alpha}{5}-\mu_{1}n\alpha]]

\displaystyle=1-\mu_{1}n\alpha[1-\frac{3(1+\varepsilon)}{50}-\lambda(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[\frac{18(1+\varepsilon)}{5}-1]],

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=1-n\beta\mu_{2}+\frac{2l^{2}n\alpha}{\mu_{2}\lambda}+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{3l^{2}n\alpha}{50\mu_{2}\lambda}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[\frac{2l^{2}n\alpha}{\mu_{2}}+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{18l^{2}n\alpha}{5\mu_{2}}]

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2l^{2}n\alpha}{\mu_{2}}[\frac{\beta\mu_{2}^{2}}{2\alpha l^{2}}-\frac{1}{\lambda}-(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{3}{100\lambda}-(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[1+(1+1/\varepsilon)\frac{9}{5}]],

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=[\frac{1}{2}+\frac{13\lambda}{10}(1-n\beta\mu_{2})]n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}+\frac{21\lambda n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

By setting
\lambda=\frac{1}{10},\varepsilon=\frac{3}{7}
and
\eta=\frac{73l^{2}}{2\mu_{2}^{2}}
and using
l^{2}\geq\mu_{1}\mu_{2}
, we conclude:

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2,

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{l^{2}n\alpha}{2\mu_{2}}\leq 1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2,

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle\leq 0.21n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3})

From the above bounds, it follows that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{k}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0})+0.21n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3}).

By taking expectations with respect to the epoch iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{j}_{0})_{j=1}^{k}
and applying the law of iterated expectation, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})]\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0})]+0.21n^{2}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3}).

By unrolling the above inequality for
K
steps, we obtain the following:

\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+0.42n\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}.

(E.27)

By setting
\alpha=\min\{1/5\eta nl,4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/\mu_{1}nK\}
and using
\eta\leq 37\kappa^{2}
, we observe that:

\displaystyle n\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16l^{2}\sigma^{2}(3+\eta^{3})}{\mu_{1}^{3}}\frac{\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{nK^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{\mu_{1}nK^{2}},

where
c>0
is a constant that is independent of
\kappa,\mu
and
\sigma^{2}
. By substituting the above into (
E.27
), we get:

\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{c\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{\mu_{1}nK^{2}}.

(E.28)

To complete the proof, we consider the following cases:

Case 1:
1/5\eta nl\leq 4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/\mu_{1}nK

In this case,
\alpha=1/5\eta nl
and hence, we get:

\displaystyle(1-n\alpha\mu_{1}/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq e^{-n\alpha\mu_{1}K/2}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0}).

Substituting into (
E.28
), we get:

\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]\leq e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{c\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{\mu_{1}nK^{2}}.

(E.29)

Case 2:
1/5\eta nl\leq 4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/(\mu_{1}nK)

In this case,
\alpha=4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/\mu_{1}nK
and we conclude that,

\displaystyle(1-n\alpha\mu_{1}/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-n\alpha\mu_{1}K/2}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq e^{-2\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})^{1/2}n^{1/2}K)}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq\frac{1}{nK^{2}}.

By substituting into (
E.28
), we get:

\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]\leq\frac{\mu_{1}+c\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{\mu_{1}nK^{2}}.

(E.30)

By taking the maximum of the right hand side of (
E.29
) and (
E.30
), we obtain the following rate, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]\leq e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{\mu_{1}+c\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{\mu_{1}nK^{2}}.

Suppressing constant terms and logarithmic factors, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]=\tilde{O}(e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}}).

∎

Bounded iterate assumption

We now demonstrate that our analysis of AGDA-RR easily adapts to the bounded iterate setting. To this end, we assume that all the iterates of AGDA-RR, i.e., the iterates
(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})
and
(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{n},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{i})
where
i\in[n]
and
k\in[K]
are contained within a compact set
\mathcal{W}
. Furthermore, we note that the
l
-smoothness of the
f_{i}
’s guarantees that the function
G_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\omega_{i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nu(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\rvert^{2}
is non-negative and continuous. Hence,
\sigma^{2}=\max_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{W}}G_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is non-negative and finite. Since
(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\in\mathcal{W}
and
(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\in\mathcal{W}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle 1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\omega_{i}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nu(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\leq\sigma^{2},

\displaystyle 1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\omega_{i}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nu(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}.

Substituting the above inequalities into Step 2 of Theorem
E.1
shows that our convergence analysis of AGDA-RR straightforwardly adapts to the bounded iterate setting. The same argument also applies to our analysis of AGDA-AS.

Convergence in terms of
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}

Let
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
denote the set of all saddle points of the objective
F
and let
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})
denote the distance of a point
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
. As we demonstrate in Appendix
E.4
, the Lyapunov function
V_{\lambda}
used in our analysis satisfies the following:

\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}\leq\max\left\{\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu_{2}}+1),\frac{4}{\lambda\mu_{2}}\right\}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}).

Using the above relation, the convergence guarantee of Theorem
E.1
can be translated to a guarantee of the form
\mathbb{E}[\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}]=\tilde{O}(e^{-\nicefrac{{K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
. Thus, the epoch iterates
\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}
of AGDA-RR converge (in expectation) to some saddle point of
F
at a rate of
\tilde{O}(e^{-\nicefrac{{K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
. A similar argument also applies to AGDA-AS.

E.3
Analysis of AGDA-AS

Theorem E.2

(Convergence of AGDA-AS)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
3
, and
4
be satisfied and let
\eta=\nicefrac{{73l^{2}}}{{2\mu^{2}_{2}}}
. Then, for any
\alpha\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{5\eta nl}}
,
\beta=\eta\alpha
, and
K\geq 1
, the iterates of AGDA-AS satisfy the following:

\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+2n^{2}\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(2+3\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}.

Setting
\alpha=\min\{1/5\eta nl,4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)/\mu_{1}nK\}
results in the following last iterate convergence guarantee which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{\mu_{1}+\hat{c}\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)}{\mu_{1}K^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}),

where
\kappa=\max\{\nicefrac{{l}}{{\mu_{1}}},\nicefrac{{l}}{{\mu_{2}}}\}
and
\hat{c}>0
is a numerical constant that is independent of
l,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and
\sigma^{2}
.

Proof.

Step 1: Epoch level update rule

We highlight that the derivation of the epoch level update rule in Step 1 of Theorem
E.1
applies to any permutations
\tau_{k},\pi_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Thus, repeating the same arguments gives us the following epoch level update rule for AGDA-AS:

\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}-n\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\alpha^{2}\mathbf{r}_{k},

(E.31)

\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0}=\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}+n\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})+\beta^{2}\mathbf{s}_{k},

(E.32)

where
\mathbf{r}_{k}
and
\mathbf{s}_{k}
are defined as follows:

\mathbf{r}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}-\alpha\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}),

(E.33)

\mathbf{s}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[\widetilde{\prod}_{t=i+2}^{n}(\mathbf{I}+\beta\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(t)})\right]\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}).

(E.34)

As before,
\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i)}
are defined as,

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{k}(i)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}+t(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}),\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\textrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\pi_{k}(i)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}f_{\pi_{k}(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}+t(\mathbf{y}^{k}_{i-1}-\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0}))\textrm{d}t.

Step 2: Uniform bound on the noise terms

Once again, we repeat the same computations as in Step 2 of Theorem
E.1
to obtain:

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert\leq le^{1/5}\left[n^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert/2+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|\right].

Defining
d_{k,i}=|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-1/i\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|
and using Young’s inequality gives us the following:

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert\leq le^{1/5}\left[n^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert/2+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}id_{k,i}\right],

(E.35)

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2l^{2}e^{2/5}\left[n^{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}/4+(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}d^{2}_{k,i}\right].

(E.36)

We proceed by using the bounded gradient variance assumption as follows:

\displaystyle(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}d^{2}_{k,i}

\displaystyle=(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{i}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\tau_{k}(j)}(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(n-1)(n\sigma^{2})\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i\leq n^{4}\sigma^{2}/2.

Substituting the above into (
E.36
) gives us the following:

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{3n^{4}l^{2}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3\sigma^{2}l^{2}n^{4}}{2}.

(E.37)

Furthermore, by Young’s inequality, we obtain,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}id_{k,i}\leq[(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i^{2}d^{2}_{k,i}]^{1/2}\leq n^{2}\sigma/\sqrt{2}.

By substituting the above into (
E.35
), we obtain the following:

\left\lvert\mathbf{r}_{k}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2n^{2}l}{3}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert+\sigma ln^{2}.

(E.38)

Performing similar computations for
\mathbf{s}_{k}
gives us the following bounds:

\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2n^{2}l}{3}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert+\sigma ln^{2},

(E.39)

\left\lvert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{3n^{4}l^{2}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3\sigma^{2}l^{2}n^{4}}{2}.

(E.40)

We highlight that the bounds (
E.11
), (
E.12
) (
E.13
) and (
E.14
) hold uniformly for any permutations
\tau_{k},\pi_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
.

Step 3: Obtaining a convergence guarantee
As before, we define
a_{k}=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-\Phi^{*}
and
b_{k}=\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})
which implies that
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k}_{0})=a_{k}+\lambda b_{k}
.

We follow the same procedure as Step 3 of Theorem
E.1
and substitute the uniform bounds (
E.11
), (
E.12
) (
E.13
) and (
E.14
) wherever appropriate to obtain the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3n^{3}\alpha^{3}l^{2}}{4}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3n^{3}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2},

(E.41)

F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\leq(\frac{3n\alpha}{2}+\frac{13}{10}ln^{2}\alpha^{2}+\frac{3n^{4}\alpha^{4}l^{3}}{8})\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{9\alpha^{3}n^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10},

(E.42)

\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k+1}_{0})\leq(1-n\beta\mu_{2})[\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0})-F(\mathbf{x}^{k+1}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})]+\frac{23n^{2}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

(E.43)

Once again, following the same steps as Step 3 of Theorem
E.1
, we conclude that,

\displaystyle a_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq a_{k}+n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}-\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3n\alpha}{100}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{k},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{k})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{3n^{3}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

(E.44)

b_{k+1}\leq(1-n\beta\mu_{2})b_{k}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})n\alpha\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})-\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}-(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{n\alpha}{2}\left\lvert\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}\\
+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{9n\alpha}{5}\left\lvert\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{k}_{0})\right\rvert^{2}+(1-n\beta\mu_{2})\frac{12n^{3}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{5}+\frac{23n^{3}\beta^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10}.

(E.45)

By setting
\lambda=\frac{1}{10}
and
\eta=\frac{73l^{2}}{2\mu_{2}^{2}}
and following the same steps as in Step 3 of Theorem
E.1
, we obtain the following update equation for
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})
:

\displaystyle V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{k+1}_{0})+n^{3}\alpha^{3}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(2+3\eta^{3}).

By unrolling the above inequality for
K
steps we obtain the following:

V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+2n^{2}\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(2+3\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}.

Since the above holds for any sequence of permutations
\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we conclude that:

\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+2n^{2}\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(2+3\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}.

(E.46)

Setting
\alpha=\min\{1/5\eta nl,4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)/\mu_{1}nK\}
and using
\eta\leq 37\kappa^{2}
, we observe that:

\displaystyle n^{2}\alpha^{2}l^{2}\sigma^{2}(2+3\eta^{3})/\mu_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16l^{2}\sigma^{2}(2+3\eta^{3})}{\mu_{1}^{3}}\frac{\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)}{K^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\hat{c}\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)}{\mu_{1}K^{2}},

where
\hat{c}>0
is a numerical constant that is independent of
\kappa,\mu
and
\sigma^{2}
. By substituting the above into (
E.46
), we get:

\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq(1-\mu_{1}n\alpha/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{\hat{c}\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)}{\mu_{1}K^{2}}.

(E.47)

Case 1:
1/5\eta nl\leq 4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/\mu_{1}nK

In this case,
\alpha=1/5\eta nl
and hence, we get:

\displaystyle(1-n\alpha\mu_{1}/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq e^{-n\alpha\mu_{1}K/2}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0}).

By substituting into (
E.47
), we get:

\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{\hat{c}\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)}{\mu_{1}K^{2}}.

(E.48)

Case 2:
1/5\eta nl\leq 4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/(\mu_{1}nK)

In this case,
\alpha=4\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})n^{1/2}K)/\mu_{1}nK
and we conclude that,

\displaystyle(1-n\alpha\mu_{1}/2)^{K}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-n\alpha\mu_{1}K/2}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq e^{-2\log(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})^{1/2}n^{1/2}K)}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\leq\frac{1}{nK^{2}}.

By substituting into (
E.47
), we get:

\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq\frac{\mu_{1}+\hat{c}\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)}{\mu_{1}K^{2}}.

(E.49)

Taking the maximum of the right hand side of (
E.48
) and (
E.49
), we obtain the following rate, which holds for any
K\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\max_{\tau_{1},\pi_{1},\ldots,\tau_{K},\pi_{K}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})\leq e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})+\frac{\mu_{1}+\hat{c}\kappa^{8}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{0})K)}{\mu_{1}K^{2}}.

Suppressing constant terms and logarithmic factors, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0})]=\tilde{O}(e^{-\frac{K}{365\kappa^{3}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}}).

∎

E.4
Convergence of AGDA-RR and AGDA-AS to a Saddle Point

In this section, we demonstrate that our convergence guarantees for AGDA-RR and AGDA-AS can be expressed in terms of squared distance to the set of saddle points of
F
, denoted as
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
. We do so by upper bounding
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}
in terms of
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z})
. We begin by establishing some basic properties of
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
as follows.

Lemma E.5

(Set of Saddle Points of 2PŁ functions)
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an
l
-smooth function satisfying Assumption
3
(with constants
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
) and let
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
denote the set of saddle points of
F
. Then
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
is a closed set and
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})=\min_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert
is a well defined continuous function of
\mathbf{z}
. Furthermore, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
, the set
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\arg\max_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{y}})
is a closed set.

Proof.

By the
l
-smoothness of
F
,
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
are Lipschitz continuous functions, which implies that
\eta(\mathbf{z})=[\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{z}),\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{z})]
is a continuous map. Furthermore, by Lemma
E.1
, the set of saddle points of
F
are exactly the set of stationary points of
F
, which means,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{*}=\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ |\ \nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F(\mathbf{z})=0,\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{z})=0\}=\eta^{-1}(\{0\}).

Hence,
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
is a closed set as it is the inverse image of the closed set
\{0\}
under a continuous map. Consequently,
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})=\min_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}^{*}}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert
is a well defined continuous function of
\mathbf{z}
.

Consider any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
. Since
F
is a 2PŁ function,
-F(\mathbf{x},.)
satisfies the (one-sided) PŁ inequality. Thus,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
if and only if
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}\ |\ \nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0\}
. By the continuity of
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}F(\mathbf{x},.)
, we conclude that
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
is a closed set.
∎

The following lemma, which is key to establishing the relation between
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z})
and
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})
, is an adaptation of Lemma A.3 of
Nouiehed et al. 2019
for 2PŁ functions.

Lemma E.6

(Lipschitzness of
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
)
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an
l
-smooth function satisfying Assumption
3
(with constants
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
). For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
, let
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\arg\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Then, for any
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
and
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})
, there exists a
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})
such that,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})\right\rvert\leq\frac{l}{2\mu_{2}}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\right\rvert.

Proof.

Consider any
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
and
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})
. Since
F
is 2PŁ with constants
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
,
-F(\mathbf{x},.)
satisfies the PŁ inequality for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
with constant
\mu_{2}
. Furthermore, by Lemma
E.5
,
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
is a closed set for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}
. Thus, by Lemma A.3 of
Nouiehed et al. 2019
, there exists a
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})
satisfying
\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})\right\rvert\leq\frac{l}{2\mu_{2}}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\right\rvert
.
∎

Equipped with the above lemmas, we can finally upper bound
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})
in terms of
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z})
.

Lemma E.7
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an
l
-smooth function satisfying Assumption
3
(with constants
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
). Define
\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
,
\Phi^{*}=\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}}\Phi(\mathbf{x})
,
\mathcal{X}^{*}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}}\Phi(\mathbf{x})
and
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\arg\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{y}}}}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Moreover, for any
\lambda>0
, define
V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=[(\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi^{*})+\lambda(\Phi(\mathbf{x})-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]
. Then the following holds for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}\leq\max\left\{\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}+1),\frac{4}{\lambda\mu_{2}}\right\}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}).

Proof.

Since the set of saddle points of
F
is non-empty and
\Phi
is a PŁ function (with constant
\mu_{1}
) as per Lemma
E.2
, we can use the same arguments as Lemma
E.5
to show that
\mathcal{X}^{*}
is a closed set.

Consider any
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Let
\mathbf{x}^{*}
be the projection of
\mathbf{x}
on the closed set
\mathcal{X}^{*}
and let
\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
be the projection of
\mathbf{y}
on the closed set (as per Lemma
E.5
)
\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
.

Applying Lemma
E.3
to
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
, we obtain,

\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi^{*}\geq\frac{\mu_{1}}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

(E.50)

Similarly, applying Lemma
E.3
to
-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, we obtain,

\Phi(\mathbf{x})-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\geq\frac{\mu_{2}}{2}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}.

(E.51)

By Lemma
E.6
, there exists a
\mathbf{y}^{*}\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{*})
satisfying,

\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{y}^{*}\right\rvert\leq\frac{l}{2\mu_{2}}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rvert.

(E.52)

Let
\mathbf{z}^{*}=(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})
. We note that since
\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
and
\mathbf{y}^{*}\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{*})
,
\mathbf{z}^{*}
is a global minimax point of
F
. Thus by Lemma
E.1
,
\mathbf{z}^{*}
is a saddle point of
F
, i.e.,
\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}^{*}
. We now proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{y}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert+\frac{l}{2\mu_{2}}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rvert.

Applying Young’s Inequality, we obtain,

\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}\left\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rvert^{2},

(E.53)

which implies that.

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}+(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}+1)\left\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}.

Substituting (
E.50
) and (
E.51
) in the above inequality, we obtain,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu_{2}}[\Phi(\mathbf{x})-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]+\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}+1)[\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}+1),\frac{4}{\lambda\mu_{2}}\right\}[(\Phi(\mathbf{x})-\Phi^{*})+\lambda(\Phi(\mathbf{x})-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))]

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}+1),\frac{4}{\lambda\mu_{2}}\right\}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}).

Since
\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}^{*}
, we conclude that,

\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}\leq\max\left\{\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}(\frac{l^{2}}{2\mu^{2}_{2}}+1),\frac{4}{\lambda\mu_{2}}\right\}V_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}).

(E.54)

∎

Using Lemma
E.7
and
E.1
, we can conclude that AGDA-RR converges in terms of
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}
as
\mathbb{E}[\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}]=\tilde{O}(e^{-\nicefrac{{K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
. Since
\mathcal{Z}^{*}
is a closed set, this implies that the final epoch iterate
\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}
of AGDA-RR converges (in expectation) to some saddle point of
F
, with the rate
\tilde{O}(e^{-\nicefrac{{K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
. Similarly, using Using Lemma
E.7
and
E.2
, we conclude that the final epoch iterate
\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0}
of AGDA-AS converges to some saddle point of
F
with the rate
\textrm{dist}(\mathbf{z}^{K+1}_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{*})^{2}=\tilde{O}(e^{-\nicefrac{{K}}{{365\kappa^{3}}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}})
.

Appendix F
Literature Review

While the convergence properties of SGD (and SGDA) with uniform sampling have been well studied for a long time, with matching upper and lower bounds of
\Theta(\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK}})
for both strongly convex minimization and strongly convex-strongly concave minimax optimization
[
44
,
13
]
(
n
is the number of components in the finite-sum objective and
K
is the number of epochs), advances in the theoretical understanding of without-replacement sampling for SGD are a fairly recent phenomenon. This is generally attributed to the unavailability of provably unbiased gradient estimates, leading to a significantly more complicated analysis that often requires sophisticated mathematical tools.

Initial progress was made by the pioneering work of
Recht and Re 2012
who proposed the
noncommutative AM-GM conjecture
, and proved that this conjecture implies the faster convergence of without-replacement SGD. Unfortunately, the conjecture was recently disproved by
Lai and Lim 2020
. Subsequent progress was made in
Gürbüzbalaban et al. 2019
, where the authors establish an asymptotic
O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}})
convergence rate for GD with Random Reshuffling (or RR) on functions of the form
F(x)=1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)
where
F
is a strongly convex quadratic and the components
f_{i}
satisfy a Hessian Lipschitz assumption. For the same function class,
Gürbüzbalaban et al. 2019
established asymptotic
O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}})
rates for Gradient Descent with Shuffle Once (SO) and Incremental Gradient (IG). The analysis in both these works relied on the following intuition, also proposed earlier in
Nedic and Bertsekas 1999
:
since sampling without replacement ensures that each component function is used exactly once within an epoch, the overall progress made by RR (and IG) over a complete epoch closely tracks that of full batch GD
. To this end, the key technique in both these works was to interpret the time evolution of the epoch iterates
\mathbf{x}^{k}_{0}
as full batch GD with added noise, and to subsequently obtain a convergence rate by determining the (asymptotic) rate of decay for the noise using Chung’s Lemma. This approach still remains a highly useful strategy for understanding SGD without replacement, and has served as a key insight for several future works such as
Haochen and Sra 2019
,
Ahn et al. 2020
. In fact, formally extending this insight to minimax optimization (and more generally, to variational inequalities) in a manner that can simultaneously handle RR, SO and adversarial shuffling, is a cornerstone of our analysis.

The first nonasymptotic guarantees for RR, which also characterize the dependence of the convergence rate on
n
, were given by
Haochen and Sra 2019
, who establish a convergence rate of
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n^{2}K^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{3}}})
assuming
K\geq C\kappa\log(nK)
for some
C>0
, for the case when
F
is a strongly convex quadratic and the components
f_{i}
are convex quadratics. Under the assumption that
F
satisfies the Polyak-Łojasiewicz inequality and the components
f_{i}
are smooth and Hessian Lipschitz,
Haochen and Sra 2019
also establish an
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n^{2}K^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{3}}})
rate assuming
K\geq C\kappa^{2}\log(nK)
. These rates imply the provable superiority of RR when
K\geq\Omega(\sqrt{n})
. Subsequent advances were made by
Nagaraj et al. 2019
, who used sophisticated Wasserstein coupling arguments to establish a rate of
O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
for RR which holds under the epoch requirement of
K\geq C\kappa^{2}\log(nK)
, assuming
F
is strongly convex and
f_{i}
are smooth and Lipschitz. This rate demonstrates the provable improvement of RR over uniform sampling as soon as the epoch requirement is satisfied.

Development of lower bounds for SGD without replacement began with
Safran and Shamir 2020
where the authors establish a lower bound of
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n^{2}K^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{3}}})
for RR on strongly convex quadratic
F
with
f_{i}
being convex quadratics, assuming constant step-sizes. Under the same assumptions,
Safran and Shamir 2020
also establish an
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
lower bound for SO and an
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}})
lower bound for IG. Further progress on lower bounds and their tightness was made in
Rajput et al. 2020
where the authors establish a
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n^{2}K^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{3}}})
convergence rate for RR on strongly convex quadratic
F
with convex quadratic
f_{i}
.
Rajput et al. 2020
also develop a lower bound of
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
for strongly convex
F
with smooth
f_{i}
, thereby establishing the tightness of the upper bounds in
Nagaraj et al. 2019
modulo logarithmic factors.

Recently, the theory of SGD without replacement has been significantly advanced by the parallel works of
Ahn et al. 2020
and
Mishchenko et al. 2020
. For the case of Polyak-Łojasiewicz
F
with smooth (but not necessarily convex)
f_{i}
,
Ahn et al. 2020
show that RR converges at an optimal rate (modulo logarithmic factors) of
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
when
K\geq C\kappa\log(n^{1/2}K)
. For the same problem class,
Mishchenko et al. 2020
obtain an anytime-valid near-optimal rate of
\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{4\kappa}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
under a bounded gradient variance assumption. This rate matches the optimal rate modulo logarithmic factors when
K\geq C\kappa\log(n^{1/2}K)
.
Ahn et al. 2020
also analyze SO for this problem class and derive
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
rates assuming
K\geq C\kappa^{2}\log(n^{1/2}K)
. The obtained rate matches the lower bound of
Safran and Shamir 2020
modulo logarithmic factors. Concurrently,
Mishchenko et al. 2020
present an analysis that covers both RR and SO for strongly convex
F
with smooth convex
f_{i}
and obtain a rate of
\tilde{O}(e^{\nicefrac{{-K}}{{4\kappa}}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{nK^{2}}})
. For IG,
Nguyen et al. 2021
obtain a rate of
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}})
assuming strongly convex
F
, smooth
f_{i}
and an epoch requirement of
K\geq 12\kappa^{2}\log(K)
. Moving beyond the PŁ class, the recent work of
Li et al. 2021
analyze RR for the case when
f_{i}
are smooth and
F
satisfies a KŁ inequality and, using variable step-sizes, establish an asymptotic convergence rate of
O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{K^{2}}})
when the KŁ exponent lies in
[0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}]
.

Recent works have also analyzed RR and SO in the federated minimization setting. Notably,
Yun et al. 2022
developed an extension of the techniques used in
Ahn et al. 2020
to analyze RR variants of minibatch and local SGD, while
Mishchenko et al. 2022
extended the approach of
Mishchenko et al. 2020
to develop federated analogs of RR and SO. Among other developments,
Tran et al. 2021
propose a variant of SGD with momentum that is amenable to theoretical analysis under without-replacement sampling. These developments are also complemented by the recent negative result of
Safran and Shamir 2021
which analyzes the condition number dependence of RR, and through an intricate lower bound analysis on convex quadratics, show that RR and SO do not significantly outperform uniform sampling unless
K
is larger than the condition number.

Despite the recent wave of developments on SGD without replacement, theoretical analysis of sampling without replacement for minimax optimization has remained relatively unexplored. To the best of our knowledge,
Yu et al. 2022
and
Maheshwari et al. 2022
are the only prior works in this domain. Both these works focus only on Random Reshuffling and are restricted to finite-sum minimax problems of the form
F(x,y)=1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x,y)
where each
f_{i}
is smooth, Lipschitz, convex-concave and has bounded domain. Under this setting,
Yu et al. 2022
proposes a stochastic proximal point method with RR that exhibits a rate of
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{nK}}})
whereas
Maheshwari et al. 2022
proposes a zeroth-order optimistic gradient method with RR that exhibits a rate of
\tilde{O}(\nicefrac{{nd^{2}}}{{K^{1/4}}})
, where
d
is the dimension of the domain. The analysis in both these works are based on an application of the Wasserstein coupling technique of
Nagaraj et al. 2019
(for the convex case) to smooth, Lipschitz convex-concave minimax optimization.

Appendix G
Experimental Details

As described in Section
5
of the main text, we perform our benchmarks on finite-sum strongly convex-strongly concave quadratic minimax games. The objective
F
and the components
f_{i}
are given by:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{y}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{y}^{T}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{y},

\displaystyle f_{i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{y}^{T}\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{u}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{y},

where
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{C}
are positive definite and
\mathbf{u}_{i}
and
\mathbf{v}_{i}
are chosen such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{u}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{i}=0
. In all our experiments, we set
n=100
and
\textrm{dim }\mathbf{x}=\textrm{dim }\mathbf{y}=25
. We randomly generate the components
f_{i}
such that 20 randomly selected components are strongly convex-strongly concave. To this end, the components
\mathbf{A}_{i}
are selected as follows. We begin by sampling a random orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}_{A}
, and then generate two
d
-dimensional vectors
\mathbf{m}_{A}
and
\delta_{A}
, such that each component of
\mathbf{m}_{A}
is uniformly sampled from
[\mu_{A},L_{A}]
whereas that of
\delta_{A}
is uniformly sampled from
[\mu_{\delta},L_{\delta}]
. Subsequently, for each
i\in[n]
, we set
\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathbf{O}_{A}\Lambda_{i}\mathbf{O}_{A}^{T}
where
\Lambda_{i}=\textrm{diag}(-\delta_{A})
or 20 randomly selected indexes and
\Lambda_{i}=\textrm{diag}(5\mathbf{m}_{A}/4+\delta_{A}/4)
for the other 80 components. A similar procedure is followed for generating the matrices
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}
. For the vectors
\mathbf{u}_{i}
, we generate a vector
\delta_{u}
with components that are uniformly sampled from
[\mu_{\delta},L_{\delta}]
. Then, for 20 randomly sampled indexes, we set
\mathbf{u}_{i}=-\delta_{u}
and for the rest, we set
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\delta_{u}/4
. The vectors
\mathbf{v}_{i}
are sampled in a similar fashion. We run each algorithm for
K=100
epochs using constant step-sizes of the form
\alpha=\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{n}}
where
\gamma
is chosen independently for each algorithm via grid search. In all our experiments, we set
\mu_{A}=\mu_{C}=0.5
,
\mu_{B}=5.0
and
\mu_{\delta}=50.0
. Furthermore, we set
L_{A}=L_{C}=2\mu_{A}
,
L_{B}=2\mu_{B}
and
L_{\delta}=2\mu_{\delta}
. We perform our experiments on a Jupyter notebook with a Python 3.7 kernel, executed on a 2.8 GHz Intel Core i7 processor with 8 GB of memory. The total running time is 40 minutes.
